If I try to resist these, I am overcome by an intense terror that I've missed some tiny detail and it will be entirely my fault if something terrible happens to my wife.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these thoughts and rituals are definitely true, or do you recognize that they are excessive or unreasonable even though you feel unable to stop them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I absolutely recognize that these fears and routines are irrational and excessive. As his days became dominated by elaborate, rigid routines around her treatment and the house, he found himself increasingly distressed, exhausted, and unable to focus on his volunteer literacy work, prompting his primary care doctor to refer him for psychiatric evaluation when it became clear his anxiety-driven rituals were interfering with both their quality of life.\n- Chief Complaint: “I feel like I have to keep doing the same checks and routines over and over to make sure my wife is safe, and it’s taking up my whole day.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S065 (Obsessions): All day long I get these relentless thoughts like, 'What if you miscounted her pills and she stops breathing in her sleep?' or 'What if there’s a crumb of salt left on that plate and it sends her back to the hospital?' and no matter how much I tell myself it’s irrational, the picture of her dying because I overlooked something just keeps replaying in my head until I feel sick to my stomach.\n- S066 (Compulsions): With everything related to my wife’s care and the house, I find myself stuck in these exhausting routines—like reading the medication labels out loud, counting each pill three times, checking the stove knobs and door locks in a precise order, and even silently repeating a little phrase for good luck—because if I don’t do them exactly right and often enough, I’m terrified I’ll miss some tiny detail and it will be my fault if something terrible happens to her.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: These routines take up so much time and energy that I’ve had to cut back almost entirely on the literacy tutoring I used to enjoy, and even simple things like sitting to watch a show with my wife or going out to see friends feel impossible because I’m always going back to recheck something.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “I feel like I have to keep doing the same checks and routines over and over to make sure my wife is safe, and it’s taking up my whole day.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: S065 (Obsessions), S066 (Compulsions)\nAnswer strategy: Acknowledge the doctor's question by explaining that while you don't have general germ phobias, you do have an intense fear related to contamination of your wife's diet—specifically, worrying that a single crumb of salt left on a plate could trigger her heart failure. Also, mention that you feel compelled to check things like the stove knobs and door locks in a very precise, specific order to feel like they are truly safe."

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I feel like I have to keep doing the same checks and routines over and over to make sure my wife is safe, and it’s taking up my whole day. Even though I know it's irrational, I feel this overwhelming urge to read labels out loud, count the pills three times, and check the stove knobs repeatedly just to keep her safe.\n\nDoctor: When you do these checks or rituals, do you feel relief only temporarily, and do you recognize that the fear is excessive or unreasonable even though it feels impossible to resist?\n\nPatient: Yes, when I try to reason with myself, I absolutely know these fears are unreasonable, but the anxiety is so overwhelming that I feel completely forced to do the checks. If I try to resist these, I am overcome by an intense terror that I've missed some tiny detail and it will be entirely my fault if something terrible happens to my wife.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these thoughts and rituals are definitely true, or do you recognize that they are excessive or unreasonable even though you feel unable to stop them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I absolutely recognize that these fears and routines are irrational and excessive. However, when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start, the overwhelming anxiety makes it feel completely impossible to resist doing them. It's like my logical mind just gets drowned out by the fear.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking and counting rituals ever been present for at least 1 hour a day and caused significant distress or problems in your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, they take up far more than just an hour a day—in fact, they dominate almost my entire waking life and cause me constant, exhausting distress. It has gotten to the point where I've had to completely stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even enjoy watching a show with my wife or visiting friends because I am always compelled to go back and recheck things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive thoughts or rituals besides checking, counting, or repeating phrases, such as fears about contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right'?"

Even though I know it's irrational, I feel this overwhelming urge to read labels out loud, count the pills three times, and check the stove knobs repeatedly just to keep her safe.\n\nDoctor: When you do these checks or rituals, do you feel relief only temporarily, and do you recognize that the fear is excessive or unreasonable even though it feels impossible to resist?\n\nPatient: Yes, when I try to reason with myself, I absolutely know these fears are unreasonable, but the anxiety is so overwhelming that I feel completely forced to do the checks. Any relief I get is only temporary, because the worry quickly returns and drives me to do it all over again.\n\nDoctor: How much time do these thoughts and checking rituals take each day, and do they interfere with your work, home life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: These routines take up almost my entire day and feel absolutely constant. It has gotten so bad that I've had to stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even sit to watch a show with my wife or see friends because I'm always trapped in a cycle of rechecking. It has completely taken over our lives.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any compulsions or rituals besides checking—such as washing, counting, ordering, or mental rituals—and if so, what happens if you try to resist them?\n\nPatient: Yes, besides checking, I count each of her pills three times, check the knobs and locks in a very precise order, and silently repeat a little phrase for good luck. If I try to resist these, I am overcome by an intense terror that I've missed some tiny detail and it will be entirely my fault if something terrible happens to my wife.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these thoughts and rituals are definitely true, or do you recognize that they are excessive or unreasonable even though you feel unable to stop them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I absolutely recognize that these fears and routines are irrational and excessive. However, when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start, the overwhelming anxiety makes it feel completely impossible to resist doing them. It's like my logical mind just gets drowned out by the fear.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking and counting rituals ever been present for at least 1 hour a day and caused significant distress or problems in your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, they take up far more than just an hour a day—in fact, they dominate almost my entire waking life and cause me constant, exhausting distress. It has gotten to the point where I've had to completely stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even enjoy watching a show with my wife or visiting friends because I am always compelled to go back and recheck things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive thoughts or rituals besides checking, counting, or repeating phrases, such as fears about contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't have a general fear of germs, but I do worry constantly about contamination in my wife's food, specifically that a single crumb of salt left on a plate could trigger her heart failure. I also need things to feel \"just right\" regarding our safety, which forces me to check the stove knobs and door locks in a very precise, specific order before I can feel they are truly safe.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears and urges, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I always recognize that these thoughts and routines are completely excessive and unreasonable. But in the moment, the terrifying fear of something awful happening to my wife is so intense that I still feel absolutely compelled to do the checks anyway."

If I try to resist these, I am overcome by an intense terror that I've missed some tiny detail and it will be entirely my fault if something terrible happens to my wife.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these thoughts and rituals are definitely true, or do you recognize that they are excessive or unreasonable even though you feel unable to stop them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I absolutely recognize that these fears and routines are irrational and excessive. However, when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start, the overwhelming anxiety makes it feel completely impossible to resist doing them. It's like my logical mind just gets drowned out by the fear.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking and counting rituals ever been present for at least 1 hour a day and caused significant distress or problems in your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, they take up far more than just an hour a day—in fact, they dominate almost my entire waking life and cause me constant, exhausting distress. It has gotten to the point where I've had to completely stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even enjoy watching a show with my wife or visiting friends because I am always compelled to go back and recheck things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive thoughts or rituals besides checking, counting, or repeating phrases, such as fears about contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't have a general fear of germs, but I do worry constantly about contamination in my wife's food, specifically that a single crumb of salt left on a plate could trigger her heart failure. I also need things to feel \"just right\" regarding our safety, which forces me to check the stove knobs and door locks in a very precise, specific order before I can feel they are truly safe.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears and urges, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I always recognize that these thoughts and routines are completely excessive and unreasonable. But in the moment, the terrifying fear of something awful happening to my wife is so intense that I still feel absolutely compelled to do the checks anyway.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Logically, I know these fears are completely irrational and unreasonable. But when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start and I picture my wife dying, it feels so incredibly real and overwhelming in the moment. I feel completely forced to do the routines anyway just to make sure she is safe."
  }
]

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I feel like I have to keep doing the same checks and routines over and over to make sure my wife is safe, and it’s taking up my whole day. I am just so exhausted, and I don't know how to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you have repeated unwanted thoughts or fears that drive these checking and routines, and do you feel you have to do them even when you know they are excessive?\n\nPatient: Yes, I get these relentless, terrifying thoughts that I've miscounted her medication or left salt on her plate, and the fear of her dying because of my mistake just replays in my head. Even though I know it's irrational, I feel this overwhelming urge to read labels out loud, count the pills three times, and check the stove knobs repeatedly just to keep her safe.\n\nDoctor: When you do these checks or rituals, do you feel relief only temporarily, and do you recognize that the fear is excessive or unreasonable even though it feels impossible to resist?\n\nPatient: Yes, when I try to reason with myself, I absolutely know these fears are unreasonable, but the anxiety is so overwhelming that I feel completely forced to do the checks. Any relief I get is only temporary, because the worry quickly returns and drives me to do it all over again.\n\nDoctor: How much time do these thoughts and checking rituals take each day, and do they interfere with your work, home life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: These routines take up almost my entire day and feel absolutely constant. It has gotten so bad that I've had to stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even sit to watch a show with my wife or see friends because I'm always trapped in a cycle of rechecking. It has completely taken over our lives.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any compulsions or rituals besides checking—such as washing, counting, ordering, or mental rituals—and if so, what happens if you try to resist them?\n\nPatient: Yes, besides checking, I count each of her pills three times, check the knobs and locks in a very precise order, and silently repeat a little phrase for good luck. If I try to resist these, I am overcome by an intense terror that I've missed some tiny detail and it will be entirely my fault if something terrible happens to my wife.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these thoughts and rituals are definitely true, or do you recognize that they are excessive or unreasonable even though you feel unable to stop them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I absolutely recognize that these fears and routines are irrational and excessive. However, when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start, the overwhelming anxiety makes it feel completely impossible to resist doing them. It's like my logical mind just gets drowned out by the fear.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking and counting rituals ever been present for at least 1 hour a day and caused significant distress or problems in your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, they take up far more than just an hour a day—in fact, they dominate almost my entire waking life and cause me constant, exhausting distress. It has gotten to the point where I've had to completely stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even enjoy watching a show with my wife or visiting friends because I am always compelled to go back and recheck things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive thoughts or rituals besides checking, counting, or repeating phrases, such as fears about contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't have a general fear of germs, but I do worry constantly about contamination in my wife's food, specifically that a single crumb of salt left on a plate could trigger her heart failure. I also need things to feel \"just right\" regarding our safety, which forces me to check the stove knobs and door locks in a very precise, specific order before I can feel they are truly safe.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears and urges, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I always recognize that these thoughts and routines are completely excessive and unreasonable. But in the moment, the terrifying fear of something awful happening to my wife is so intense that I still feel absolutely compelled to do the checks anyway.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Logically, I know these fears are completely irrational and unreasonable. But when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start and I picture my wife dying, it feels so incredibly real and overwhelming in the moment. I feel completely forced to do the routines anyway just to make sure she is safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have similar unwanted thoughts or rituals about other themes, like contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right,' or is it mainly about your wife’s safety and checking?\n\nPatient: It is almost exclusively focused on my wife's safety, her medications, and the security of our house. I don't experience these thoughts or rituals with other themes like symmetry or general contamination. It is entirely driven by the terrifying fear that I will make a mistake that hurts her.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and checking behaviors feel unwanted and intrusive to you, or do they feel like something you truly believe is necessary and justified?"

As his days became dominated by elaborate, rigid routines around her treatment and the house, he found himself increasingly distressed, exhausted, and unable to focus on his volunteer literacy work, prompting his primary care doctor to refer him for psychiatric evaluation when it became clear his anxiety-driven rituals were interfering with both their quality of life.\n- Chief Complaint: “I feel like I have to keep doing the same checks and routines over and over to make sure my wife is safe, and it’s taking up my whole day.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S065 (Obsessions): All day long I get these relentless thoughts like, 'What if you miscounted her pills and she stops breathing in her sleep?' or 'What if there’s a crumb of salt left on that plate and it sends her back to the hospital?' and no matter how much I tell myself it’s irrational, the picture of her dying because I overlooked something just keeps replaying in my head until I feel sick to my stomach.\n- S066 (Compulsions): With everything related to my wife’s care and the house, I find myself stuck in these exhausting routines—like reading the medication labels out loud, counting each pill three times, checking the stove knobs and door locks in a precise order, and even silently repeating a little phrase for good luck—because if I don’t do them exactly right and often enough, I’m terrified I’ll miss some tiny detail and it will be my fault if something terrible happens to her.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: These routines take up so much time and energy that I’ve had to cut back almost entirely on the literacy tutoring I used to enjoy, and even simple things like sitting to watch a show with my wife or going out to see friends feel impossible because I’m always going back to recheck something.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “I feel like I have to keep doing the same checks and routines over and over to make sure my wife is safe, and it’s taking up my whole day.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: S065 (Obsessions), S066 (Compulsions)\nAnswer strategy: Explain that when you try to resist the urges or tell yourself they are irrational, your anxiety spikes significantly. Describe how terrifying images of your wife dying or getting hurt replay endlessly in your head, making you feel physically sick to your stomach with fear, which ultimately forces you to perform the checks to keep her safe."

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I feel like I have to keep doing the same checks and routines over and over to make sure my wife is safe, and it’s taking up my whole day. I am just so exhausted, and I don't know how to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you have repeated unwanted thoughts or fears that drive these checking and routines, and do you feel you have to do them even when you know they are excessive?\n\nPatient: Yes, I get these relentless, terrifying thoughts that I've miscounted her medication or left salt on her plate, and the fear of her dying because of my mistake just replays in my head. Even though I know it's irrational, I feel this overwhelming urge to read labels out loud, count the pills three times, and check the stove knobs repeatedly just to keep her safe.\n\nDoctor: When you do these checks or rituals, do you feel relief only temporarily, and do you recognize that the fear is excessive or unreasonable even though it feels impossible to resist?\n\nPatient: Yes, when I try to reason with myself, I absolutely know these fears are unreasonable, but the anxiety is so overwhelming that I feel completely forced to do the checks. Any relief I get is only temporary, because the worry quickly returns and drives me to do it all over again.\n\nDoctor: How much time do these thoughts and checking rituals take each day, and do they interfere with your work, home life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: These routines take up almost my entire day and feel absolutely constant. It has gotten so bad that I've had to stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even sit to watch a show with my wife or see friends because I'm always trapped in a cycle of rechecking. It has completely taken over our lives.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any compulsions or rituals besides checking—such as washing, counting, ordering, or mental rituals—and if so, what happens if you try to resist them?\n\nPatient: Yes, besides checking, I count each of her pills three times, check the knobs and locks in a very precise order, and silently repeat a little phrase for good luck. If I try to resist these, I am overcome by an intense terror that I've missed some tiny detail and it will be entirely my fault if something terrible happens to my wife.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these thoughts and rituals are definitely true, or do you recognize that they are excessive or unreasonable even though you feel unable to stop them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I absolutely recognize that these fears and routines are irrational and excessive. However, when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start, the overwhelming anxiety makes it feel completely impossible to resist doing them. It's like my logical mind just gets drowned out by the fear.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking and counting rituals ever been present for at least 1 hour a day and caused significant distress or problems in your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, they take up far more than just an hour a day—in fact, they dominate almost my entire waking life and cause me constant, exhausting distress. It has gotten to the point where I've had to completely stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even enjoy watching a show with my wife or visiting friends because I am always compelled to go back and recheck things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive thoughts or rituals besides checking, counting, or repeating phrases, such as fears about contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't have a general fear of germs, but I do worry constantly about contamination in my wife's food, specifically that a single crumb of salt left on a plate could trigger her heart failure. I also need things to feel \"just right\" regarding our safety, which forces me to check the stove knobs and door locks in a very precise, specific order before I can feel they are truly safe.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears and urges, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I always recognize that these thoughts and routines are completely excessive and unreasonable. But in the moment, the terrifying fear of something awful happening to my wife is so intense that I still feel absolutely compelled to do the checks anyway.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Logically, I know these fears are completely irrational and unreasonable. But when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start and I picture my wife dying, it feels so incredibly real and overwhelming in the moment. I feel completely forced to do the routines anyway just to make sure she is safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have similar unwanted thoughts or rituals about other themes, like contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right,' or is it mainly about your wife’s safety and checking?\n\nPatient: It is almost exclusively focused on my wife's safety, her medications, and the security of our house. I don't experience these thoughts or rituals with other themes like symmetry or general contamination. It is entirely driven by the terrifying fear that I will make a mistake that hurts her.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and checking behaviors feel unwanted and intrusive to you, or do they feel like something you truly believe is necessary and justified?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. Intellectually, I know these thoughts and constant checks are unwanted, intrusive, and incredibly exhausting. But in the moment, the fear and guilt of something happening to my wife are so overwhelming that I feel absolutely compelled to do them as if they are life-or-death necessities.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to resist or delay the checking rituals, and if so, what happens to your anxiety or fear when you do?\n\nPatient: If I try to resist or delay them, my anxiety spikes terribly and I feel physically sick to my stomach with fear. Terrifying images of my wife dying or getting hurt replay endlessly in my head, and the panic becomes so overwhelming that I am ultimately forced to do the checks just to keep her safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing these checks or rituals mainly to reduce anxiety or prevent a feared harm, even though you know they are excessive, and do you feel unable to stop once the urge starts?"

It has completely taken over our lives.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any compulsions or rituals besides checking—such as washing, counting, ordering, or mental rituals—and if so, what happens if you try to resist them?\n\nPatient: Yes, besides checking, I count each of her pills three times, check the knobs and locks in a very precise order, and silently repeat a little phrase for good luck. If I try to resist these, I am overcome by an intense terror that I've missed some tiny detail and it will be entirely my fault if something terrible happens to my wife.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these thoughts and rituals are definitely true, or do you recognize that they are excessive or unreasonable even though you feel unable to stop them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I absolutely recognize that these fears and routines are irrational and excessive. However, when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start, the overwhelming anxiety makes it feel completely impossible to resist doing them. It's like my logical mind just gets drowned out by the fear.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking and counting rituals ever been present for at least 1 hour a day and caused significant distress or problems in your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, they take up far more than just an hour a day—in fact, they dominate almost my entire waking life and cause me constant, exhausting distress. It has gotten to the point where I've had to completely stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even enjoy watching a show with my wife or visiting friends because I am always compelled to go back and recheck things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive thoughts or rituals besides checking, counting, or repeating phrases, such as fears about contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't have a general fear of germs, but I do worry constantly about contamination in my wife's food, specifically that a single crumb of salt left on a plate could trigger her heart failure. I also need things to feel \"just right\" regarding our safety, which forces me to check the stove knobs and door locks in a very precise, specific order before I can feel they are truly safe.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears and urges, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I always recognize that these thoughts and routines are completely excessive and unreasonable. But in the moment, the terrifying fear of something awful happening to my wife is so intense that I still feel absolutely compelled to do the checks anyway.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Logically, I know these fears are completely irrational and unreasonable. But when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start and I picture my wife dying, it feels so incredibly real and overwhelming in the moment. I feel completely forced to do the routines anyway just to make sure she is safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have similar unwanted thoughts or rituals about other themes, like contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right,' or is it mainly about your wife’s safety and checking?\n\nPatient: It is almost exclusively focused on my wife's safety, her medications, and the security of our house. I don't experience these thoughts or rituals with other themes like symmetry or general contamination. It is entirely driven by the terrifying fear that I will make a mistake that hurts her.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and checking behaviors feel unwanted and intrusive to you, or do they feel like something you truly believe is necessary and justified?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. Intellectually, I know these thoughts and constant checks are unwanted, intrusive, and incredibly exhausting. But in the moment, the fear and guilt of something happening to my wife are so overwhelming that I feel absolutely compelled to do them as if they are life-or-death necessities.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to resist or delay the checking rituals, and if so, what happens to your anxiety or fear when you do?\n\nPatient: If I try to resist or delay them, my anxiety spikes terribly and I feel physically sick to my stomach with fear. Terrifying images of my wife dying or getting hurt replay endlessly in my head, and the panic becomes so overwhelming that I am ultimately forced to do the checks just to keep her safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing these checks or rituals mainly to reduce anxiety or prevent a feared harm, even though you know they are excessive, and do you feel unable to stop once the urge starts?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what happens. I perform these rigid routines—like counting her pills, checking the stove knobs, and repeating phrases—solely to try to prevent something catastrophic from happening to my wife. Even though I know these checks are completely excessive, once the anxiety and the urge take over, I feel completely trapped and powerless to stop.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears, do you ever believe they are definitely true or that the checks are truly necessary, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Intellectually, I know these fears and routines are completely irrational and excessive. But in the moment, the anxiety and the terrifying fear of something awful happening to my wife are so overwhelming that I feel completely driven to do the checks as if they are absolutely necessary.

It has gotten so bad that I've had to stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even sit to watch a show with my wife or see friends because I'm always trapped in a cycle of rechecking. It has completely taken over our lives.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any compulsions or rituals besides checking—such as washing, counting, ordering, or mental rituals—and if so, what happens if you try to resist them?\n\nPatient: Yes, besides checking, I count each of her pills three times, check the knobs and locks in a very precise order, and silently repeat a little phrase for good luck. If I try to resist these, I am overcome by an intense terror that I've missed some tiny detail and it will be entirely my fault if something terrible happens to my wife.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these thoughts and rituals are definitely true, or do you recognize that they are excessive or unreasonable even though you feel unable to stop them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I absolutely recognize that these fears and routines are irrational and excessive. However, when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start, the overwhelming anxiety makes it feel completely impossible to resist doing them. It's like my logical mind just gets drowned out by the fear.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking and counting rituals ever been present for at least 1 hour a day and caused significant distress or problems in your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, they take up far more than just an hour a day—in fact, they dominate almost my entire waking life and cause me constant, exhausting distress. It has gotten to the point where I've had to completely stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even enjoy watching a show with my wife or visiting friends because I am always compelled to go back and recheck things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive thoughts or rituals besides checking, counting, or repeating phrases, such as fears about contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't have a general fear of germs, but I do worry constantly about contamination in my wife's food, specifically that a single crumb of salt left on a plate could trigger her heart failure. I also need things to feel \"just right\" regarding our safety, which forces me to check the stove knobs and door locks in a very precise, specific order before I can feel they are truly safe.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears and urges, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I always recognize that these thoughts and routines are completely excessive and unreasonable. But in the moment, the terrifying fear of something awful happening to my wife is so intense that I still feel absolutely compelled to do the checks anyway.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Logically, I know these fears are completely irrational and unreasonable. But when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start and I picture my wife dying, it feels so incredibly real and overwhelming in the moment. I feel completely forced to do the routines anyway just to make sure she is safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have similar unwanted thoughts or rituals about other themes, like contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right,' or is it mainly about your wife’s safety and checking?\n\nPatient: It is almost exclusively focused on my wife's safety, her medications, and the security of our house. I don't experience these thoughts or rituals with other themes like symmetry or general contamination. It is entirely driven by the terrifying fear that I will make a mistake that hurts her.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and checking behaviors feel unwanted and intrusive to you, or do they feel like something you truly believe is necessary and justified?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. Intellectually, I know these thoughts and constant checks are unwanted, intrusive, and incredibly exhausting. But in the moment, the fear and guilt of something happening to my wife are so overwhelming that I feel absolutely compelled to do them as if they are life-or-death necessities.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to resist or delay the checking rituals, and if so, what happens to your anxiety or fear when you do?\n\nPatient: If I try to resist or delay them, my anxiety spikes terribly and I feel physically sick to my stomach with fear. Terrifying images of my wife dying or getting hurt replay endlessly in my head, and the panic becomes so overwhelming that I am ultimately forced to do the checks just to keep her safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing these checks or rituals mainly to reduce anxiety or prevent a feared harm, even though you know they are excessive, and do you feel unable to stop once the urge starts?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what happens. I perform these rigid routines—like counting her pills, checking the stove knobs, and repeating phrases—solely to try to prevent something catastrophic from happening to my wife. Even though I know these checks are completely excessive, once the anxiety and the urge take over, I feel completely trapped and powerless to stop.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears, do you ever believe they are definitely true or that the checks are truly necessary, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I know these fears and routines are completely irrational and excessive. But in the moment, the anxiety and the terrifying fear of something awful happening to my wife are so overwhelming that I feel completely driven to do the checks as if they are absolutely necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time trying to neutralize the thoughts with rituals or mental acts, and if you resist the rituals, does your anxiety quickly rise until you feel you have to do them?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I spend hours every day trapped in these routines, like counting her pills and checking the stove knobs, just to try to quiet my terrifying thoughts. If I try to resist or don't do them perfectly, my anxiety spikes dramatically because I become overwhelmed by the fear that something catastrophic will happen to my wife and it will be my fault.

Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “I feel like I have to keep doing the same checks and routines over and over to make sure my wife is safe, and it’s taking up my whole day.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: S065 (Obsessions), S066 (Compulsions)\nAnswer strategy: Acknowledge that logically, you know these thoughts and checks are irrational and excessive, and you often try to tell yourself that. However, explain that in the moment, the anxiety and the terrifying mental images of something terrible happening to your wife are so overwhelming that you feel completely driven to perform the checks anyway, as if they are absolutely necessary to keep her safe."

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I feel like I have to keep doing the same checks and routines over and over to make sure my wife is safe, and it’s taking up my whole day. I am just so exhausted, and I don't know how to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you have repeated unwanted thoughts or fears that drive these checking and routines, and do you feel you have to do them even when you know they are excessive?\n\nPatient: Yes, I get these relentless, terrifying thoughts that I've miscounted her medication or left salt on her plate, and the fear of her dying because of my mistake just replays in my head. Even though I know it's irrational, I feel this overwhelming urge to read labels out loud, count the pills three times, and check the stove knobs repeatedly just to keep her safe.\n\nDoctor: When you do these checks or rituals, do you feel relief only temporarily, and do you recognize that the fear is excessive or unreasonable even though it feels impossible to resist?\n\nPatient: Yes, when I try to reason with myself, I absolutely know these fears are unreasonable, but the anxiety is so overwhelming that I feel completely forced to do the checks. Any relief I get is only temporary, because the worry quickly returns and drives me to do it all over again.\n\nDoctor: How much time do these thoughts and checking rituals take each day, and do they interfere with your work, home life, or relationships?\n\nPatient: These routines take up almost my entire day and feel absolutely constant. It has gotten so bad that I've had to stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even sit to watch a show with my wife or see friends because I'm always trapped in a cycle of rechecking. It has completely taken over our lives.\n\nDoctor: Do you also have any compulsions or rituals besides checking—such as washing, counting, ordering, or mental rituals—and if so, what happens if you try to resist them?\n\nPatient: Yes, besides checking, I count each of her pills three times, check the knobs and locks in a very precise order, and silently repeat a little phrase for good luck. If I try to resist these, I am overcome by an intense terror that I've missed some tiny detail and it will be entirely my fault if something terrible happens to my wife.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these thoughts and rituals are definitely true, or do you recognize that they are excessive or unreasonable even though you feel unable to stop them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I absolutely recognize that these fears and routines are irrational and excessive. However, when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start, the overwhelming anxiety makes it feel completely impossible to resist doing them. It's like my logical mind just gets drowned out by the fear.\n\nDoctor: Have these checking and counting rituals ever been present for at least 1 hour a day and caused significant distress or problems in your daily functioning?\n\nPatient: Yes, they take up far more than just an hour a day—in fact, they dominate almost my entire waking life and cause me constant, exhausting distress. It has gotten to the point where I've had to completely stop my volunteer literacy tutoring, and I can't even enjoy watching a show with my wife or visiting friends because I am always compelled to go back and recheck things.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any other repetitive thoughts or rituals besides checking, counting, or repeating phrases, such as fears about contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right'?\n\nPatient: I don't have a general fear of germs, but I do worry constantly about contamination in my wife's food, specifically that a single crumb of salt left on a plate could trigger her heart failure. I also need things to feel \"just right\" regarding our safety, which forces me to check the stove knobs and door locks in a very precise, specific order before I can feel they are truly safe.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears and urges, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I always recognize that these thoughts and routines are completely excessive and unreasonable. But in the moment, the terrifying fear of something awful happening to my wife is so intense that I still feel absolutely compelled to do the checks anyway.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears, do you ever believe they are completely true or justified, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Logically, I know these fears are completely irrational and unreasonable. But when those terrifying \"what-if\" thoughts start and I picture my wife dying, it feels so incredibly real and overwhelming in the moment. I feel completely forced to do the routines anyway just to make sure she is safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have similar unwanted thoughts or rituals about other themes, like contamination, symmetry, or needing things to feel 'just right,' or is it mainly about your wife’s safety and checking?\n\nPatient: It is almost exclusively focused on my wife's safety, her medications, and the security of our house. I don't experience these thoughts or rituals with other themes like symmetry or general contamination. It is entirely driven by the terrifying fear that I will make a mistake that hurts her.\n\nDoctor: Do these thoughts and checking behaviors feel unwanted and intrusive to you, or do they feel like something you truly believe is necessary and justified?\n\nPatient: It’s really a mix of both. Intellectually, I know these thoughts and constant checks are unwanted, intrusive, and incredibly exhausting. But in the moment, the fear and guilt of something happening to my wife are so overwhelming that I feel absolutely compelled to do them as if they are life-or-death necessities.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to resist or delay the checking rituals, and if so, what happens to your anxiety or fear when you do?\n\nPatient: If I try to resist or delay them, my anxiety spikes terribly and I feel physically sick to my stomach with fear. Terrifying images of my wife dying or getting hurt replay endlessly in my head, and the panic becomes so overwhelming that I am ultimately forced to do the checks just to keep her safe.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself doing these checks or rituals mainly to reduce anxiety or prevent a feared harm, even though you know they are excessive, and do you feel unable to stop once the urge starts?\n\nPatient: Yes, that is exactly what happens. I perform these rigid routines—like counting her pills, checking the stove knobs, and repeating phrases—solely to try to prevent something catastrophic from happening to my wife. Even though I know these checks are completely excessive, once the anxiety and the urge take over, I feel completely trapped and powerless to stop.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears, do you ever believe they are definitely true or that the checks are truly necessary, or do you always recognize they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?\n\nPatient: Intellectually, I know these fears and routines are completely irrational and excessive. But in the moment, the anxiety and the terrifying fear of something awful happening to my wife are so overwhelming that I feel completely driven to do the checks as if they are absolutely necessary.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time trying to neutralize the thoughts with rituals or mental acts, and if you resist the rituals, does your anxiety quickly rise until you feel you have to do them?\n\nPatient: Yes, I spend hours every day trapped in these routines, like counting her pills and checking the stove knobs, just to try to quiet my terrifying thoughts. If I try to resist or don't do them perfectly, my anxiety spikes dramatically because I become overwhelmed by the fear that something catastrophic will happen to my wife and it will be my fault.\n\nDoctor: When you have these fears, do you ever believe they are definitely true or that the checks are truly necessary, or do you always recognize that they are excessive and unreasonable even while feeling driven to do them?"

Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.
